If 1... Rg1+, then 2. Kb2 (back to where we were) or 2. Ka2 (blocking pawn).
Wait, if White is at a1, and Black plays 1... Rg1+, White must move.
b2a1 is essentially a temporary block.

Thus, b2a3 seems more active. It forces the issue with the pawn immediately.

Wait, let me double check the board again.
Rank 2: ♟ ♔ ⭘ ⭘ ⭘ ⭘ ♜ ⭘
a2: Black Pawn
b2: White King
g2: Black Rook

Legal moves provided: b2c3,b2b3,b2a3,b2c1,b2a1.
These are all moves that get out of the check from the Rook at g2.

Let's re-examine b2a3.
King at a3, Pawn at a2.
White attacks the pawn.
If Black plays 1... a1=Q, then 2. Kxa1.
If Black plays 1... Ra2? (Rook can't go to a2 because Pawn is there).
If Black plays 1... Rg1, then 2. Kxa2.
If Black plays 1... Rh2, then 2. Kxa2.
It seems b2a3 is the most direct way to deal with the pawn.

Is there any reason to prefer b2c3, b2b3, b2c1 or b2a1?
b2c1: Moves away from the pawn. Black promotes 1... a1=Q.
b2b3: Moves away from the pawn. Black promotes 1... a1=Q.
b2c3: Moves away from the pawn. Black promotes 1... a1=Q.
b2a1: Blocks the pawn, but allows 1... Rg1+. Then what? 2. Kb2 (back to start) or 2. Ka2 (attacking the pawn).
Wait, 1. b2a1 Rg1+ 2. Ka2. Now we are at the same position as 1. b2a3.
But 1. b2a3 reaches that state immediately.

So, b2a3 is the most logical move.

One more thing: Is the King on b2 checked by anything else?
Pawn on a2: Attacks b1 and (if it was a white pawn) b3. But it's a black pawn at a2. It attacks b1.
So the King at b2 is not checked by the pawn at a2.
The Rook at g2 is the only piece checking the King.

Conclusion: b2a3 is the strongest move because it immediately threatens to capture the advanced pawn and prevents immediate promotion.
